> # 🗣️ A BRILLIANT ACT OF SEDITION, DUDE, WHERE'S MY COUNTRY? REVEALS: (Part: 전체)
>
> ## 🎯 학습 개요 (Learning Overview)
> - **주요 학습 테마**: 정치 비판, 사회 문제 제기, 설득 및 주장, 비유적 표현
> - **핵심 수록 표현 수**: 15개
> - **학습 포인트**:
> - 비판적 시각을 담은 표현과 설득력 있는 어조를 익힙니다.
> - 구어체와 격식체 표현의 혼용을 통해 다양한 상황에 맞는 어휘를 학습합니다.
> - 사회, 정치적 이슈를 다루는 데 필요한 핵심 용어와 그 뉘앙스를 이해합니다.
>
> ## 📚 무손실 핵심 표현 & 패턴 마스터 (Core Expressions & Patterns)
>
> ### 🔹 1. `the powers-that-be` : 실세, 권력자들
> - **💡 뉘앙스 & 상황 맥락**: 특정 조직이나 국가 내에서 실질적인 권력을 쥐고 있는 사람들을 지칭할 때 사용합니다. 종종 비판적이거나 냉소적인 뉘앙스를 포함하며, 일반 대중이 직접 대면하기 어려운 '보이지 않는 손' 같은 느낌을 줍니다.
> - **🔊 발음 & 연음 팁 (Pronunciation Tip)**: 'powers-that-be'는 자연스럽게 이어서 발음하며, 'that-be' 부분에서 약하게 연결됩니다. 'be'에 약간의 강세가 올 수 있습니다.
> - **📝 실전 예문 (Real Examples)**:
> - 🇺🇸 `The powers-that-be decided to cut funding for the project.`
> - 🇰🇷 실세들이 그 프로젝트의 자금 지원을 중단하기로 결정했다.
> - 🇺🇸 `It's hard to fight against the powers-that-be when they control everything.`
> - 🇰🇷 모든 것을 통제하는 권력자들에 맞서 싸우는 것은 어렵다.
>
> ### 🔹 2. `succeeded in ignoring—and then silencing—` : 무시하고, 이어서 침묵시키는 데 성공하다
> - **💡 뉘앙스 & 상황 맥락**: 어떤 시도나 노력이 성공했음을 나타내며, 특히 부정적인 맥락에서 '무시하고 잠재우는' 행위에 대한 비판적 시각을 담고 있습니다. '—and then—' 구문은 시간적 순서와 함께 강조의 의미를 더합니다.
> - **🔊 발음 & 연음 팁 (Pronunciation Tip)**: 'succeeded in'은 'succeeded-in'처럼 연음되고, 'silencing' 뒤의 'the'는 약하게 발음됩니다.
> - **📝 실전 예문 (Real Examples)**:
> - 🇺🇸 `The company succeeded in ignoring—and then silencing—the workers' complaints.`
> - 🇰🇷 그 회사는 노동자들의 불만을 무시하고, 이어서 침묵시키는 데 성공했다.
> - 🇺🇸 `Despite widespread criticism, the government succeeded in ignoring—and then silencing—the opposition.`
> - 🇰🇷 광범위한 비판에도 불구하고, 정부는 반대 의견을 무시하고, 이어서 침묵시키는 데 성공했다.
>
> ### 🔹 3. `widespread dissent over war` : 전쟁에 대한 광범위한 반대 의견
> - **💡 뉘앙스 & 상황 맥락**: 'widespread'는 '널리 퍼진', '광범위한'을 의미하며, 'dissent'는 '반대 의견'이나 '불찬성'을 뜻합니다. 특정 정책이나 결정에 대한 대중의 강력한 반발을 나타낼 때 사용됩니다.
> - **🔊 발음 & 연음 팁 (Pronunciation Tip)**: 'widespread'의 's'와 'dissent'의 'd'가 연결되는 느낌으로 발음됩니다. 'over'는 약하게 발음될 수 있습니다.
> - **📝 실전 예문 (Real Examples)**:
> - 🇺🇸 `There was widespread dissent over the new environmental regulations.`
> - 🇰🇷 새로운 환경 규제에 대한 광범위한 반대 의견이 있었다.
> - 🇺🇸 `The decision led to widespread dissent among the students.`
> - 🇰🇷 그 결정은 학생들 사이에서 광범위한 반대 의견을 불러일으켰다.
>
> ### 🔹 4. `outed the commander in chief for his fictitious presidency and his fictitious war` : 총사령관의 허구적인 대통령직과 허구적인 전쟁을 폭로했다
> - **💡 뉘앙스 & 상황 맥락**: 'out'은 '폭로하다', '정체를 드러내다'라는 의미로, 특히 숨겨진 사실이나 부정적인 면을 공개할 때 사용됩니다. 'commander in chief'는 군 통수권자를 의미하며, 'fictitious'는 '허구적인', '가상의'라는 비판적 의미를 강조합니다.
> - **🔊 발음 & 연음 팁 (Pronunciation Tip)**: 'outed the'는 'outed-thuh'처럼 연음되고, 'commander in chief'는 각 단어에 강세를 주어 또렷하게 발음합니다.
> - **📝 실전 예문 (Real Examples)**:
> - 🇺🇸 `The whistleblower outed the CEO for his fraudulent business practices.`
> - 🇰🇷 내부 고발자는 CEO의 사기성 사업 관행을 폭로했다.
> - 🇺🇸 `She outed her colleague for taking credit for her work.`
> - 🇰🇷 그녀는 자신의 일을 가로챈 동료를 폭로했다.
>
> ### 🔹 5. `remarkably become the accepted truth of the land` : 놀랍게도 그 땅의 받아들여진 진실이 되었다
> - **💡 뉘앙스 & 상황 맥락**: 'remarkably'는 '놀랍게도'라는 뜻으로, 예상치 못한 변화나 결과에 대한 놀라움을 표현합니다. 'accepted truth'는 많은 사람들이 사실로 받아들이는 것을 의미하며, 'of the land'는 '그 나라 전체의'라는 광범위한 의미를 더합니다.
> - **🔊 발음 & 연음 팁 (Pronunciation Tip)**: 'remarkably'의 'k'와 'become'의 'b'가 부드럽게 이어집니다. 'truth of the'는 'truth-uv-thuh'처럼 연음될 수 있습니다.
> - **📝 실전 예문 (Real Examples)**:
> - 🇺🇸 `His theory has remarkably become the accepted truth of the scientific community.`
> - 🇰🇷 그의 이론은 놀랍게도 과학계의 받아들여진 진실이 되었다.
> - 🇺🇸 `What was once a conspiracy theory has remarkably become the accepted truth of the land.`
> - 🇰🇷 한때 음모론이었던 것이 놀랍게도 그 나라의 받아들여진 진실이 되었다.
>
> ### 🔹 6. `the scourge of Stupid White Men everywhere` : 모든 곳의 멍청한 백인 남자들의 골칫거리/재앙
> - **💡 뉘앙스 & 상황 맥락**: 'scourge'는 '재앙', '골칫거리', '징벌'을 의미하며, 특정 대상에게 큰 고통이나 문제를 일으키는 존재를 비유적으로 표현할 때 사용됩니다. 여기서는 마이클 무어가 특정 집단에게는 불편하고 비판적인 존재임을 강조합니다.
> - **🔊 발음 & 연음 팁 (Pronunciation Tip)**: 'scourge'는 '스컬지'로 발음하며, 'everywhere'는 '에브리웨어'로 자연스럽게 이어집니다.
> - **📝 실전 예문 (Real Examples)**:
> - 🇺🇸 `He became the scourge of corrupt politicians.`
> - 🇰🇷 그는 부패한 정치인들의 골칫거리가 되었다.
> - 🇺🇸 `The new virus was the scourge of the entire region.`
> - 🇰🇷 그 새로운 바이러스는 지역 전체의 재앙이었다.
>
> ### 🔹 7. `taken on fat cats, gun nuts, lying politicians` : 부유층, 총기 옹호자들, 거짓말하는 정치인들을 상대했다
> - **💡 뉘앙스 & 상황 맥락**: 'take on'은 '~와 맞서 싸우다', '~을 상대하다'라는 의미의 구동사입니다. 여기서는 마이클 무어가 사회의 다양한 문제적 집단에 대해 비판적인 입장을 취하고 있음을 보여줍니다. 'fat cats'는 부유하고 탐욕스러운 사람들을 비하하는 표현입니다.
> - **🔊 발음 & 연음 팁 (Pronunciation Tip)**: 'taken on'은 '테이큰-온'처럼 연음되고, 각 대상(fat cats, gun nuts, lying politicians)은 명확하게 구분하여 발음합니다.
> - **📝 실전 예문 (Real Examples)**:
> - 🇺🇸 `The activist group decided to take on the powerful corporation.`
> - 🇰🇷 그 활동가 단체는 강력한 기업과 맞서 싸우기로 결정했다.
> - 🇺🇸 `It's brave of him to take on such controversial issues.`
> - 🇰🇷 그가 그렇게 논란이 많은 문제들을 상대하는 것은 용감하다.
>
> ### 🔹 8. `a wake-up call, a kick in the mental backside` : 경종, 정신적 일침
> - **💡 뉘앙스 & 상황 맥락**: 'wake-up call'은 '경종', '각성시키는 계기'를 의미하며, 'kick in the mental backside'는 비격식적인 표현으로 '정신을 차리게 하는 일침'을 뜻합니다. 둘 다 사람들에게 현실을 직시하고 변화를 촉구하는 메시지를 전달할 때 사용됩니다.
> - **🔊 발음 & 연음 팁 (Pronunciation Tip)**: 'wake-up call'은 '웨이크업 콜'로, 'kick in the'는 '킥-인-더'처럼 연음됩니다.
> - **📝 실전 예문 (Real Examples)**:
> - 🇺🇸 `The economic crisis was a wake-up call for many businesses.`
> - 🇰🇷 경제 위기는 많은 기업들에게 경종이 되었다.
> - 🇺🇸 `His harsh words were a much-needed kick in the mental backside for me.`
> - 🇰🇷 그의 가혹한 말은 나에게 꼭 필요한 정신적 일침이었다.
>
> ### 🔹 9. `daring to ask the most urgent question of these perilous times` : 이 위험한 시대의 가장 긴급한 질문을 감히 묻다
> - **💡 뉘앙스 & 상황 맥락**: 'daring to'는 '감히 ~하다'라는 의미로, 용기 있는 행동이나 도전을 강조합니다. 'urgent question'은 '긴급한 질문', 'perilous times'는 '위험한 시대'를 뜻하며, 현 상황의 심각성을 부각합니다.
> - **🔊 발음 & 연음 팁 (Pronunciation Tip)**: 'daring to ask'는 '데어링 투 애스크'처럼 자연스럽게 이어지고, 'perilous'는 '페릴러스'로 발음합니다.
> - **📝 실전 예문 (Real Examples)**:
> - 🇺🇸 `She was daring to challenge the traditional views.`
> - 🇰🇷 그녀는 감히 전통적인 견해에 도전했다.
> - 🇺🇸 `Addressing climate change is the most urgent question of our times.`
> - 🇰🇷 기후 변화에 대처하는 것이 우리 시대의 가장 긴급한 질문이다.
>
> ### 🔹 10. `Regime Change` : 정권 교체
> - **💡 뉘앙스 & 상황 맥락**: 특정 국가의 정부나 정치 체제를 교체하려는 목표를 의미하는 정치 용어입니다. 이 책에서는 부시 행정부 교체에 대한 저자의 의지를 나타내며, 강한 정치적 메시지를 담고 있습니다.
> - **🔊 발음 & 연음 팁 (Pronunciation Tip)**: 'Regime'은 '레짐'으로 발음하며, 'Change'와 함께 명확하게 발음합니다.
> - **📝 실전 예문 (Real Examples)**:
> - 🇺🇸 `The international community called for a peaceful regime change.`
> - 🇰🇷 국제 사회는 평화로운 정권 교체를 요구했다.
> - 🇺🇸 `His mission was to bring about a regime change in the corrupt country.`
> - 🇰🇷 그의 임무는 부패한 나라에서 정권 교체를 이끌어내는 것이었다.
>
> ### 🔹 11. `slithered into the White House on tracks greased by his daddy's oil buddies` : 아버지의 석유 재벌 친구들이 깔아준 길을 타고 백악관으로 기어들어갔다
> - **💡 뉘앙스 & 상황 맥락**: 'slithered'는 '미끄러지듯 움직이다', '기어들어가다'라는 뜻으로, 부정적인 뉘앙스를 강하게 풍깁니다. 'tracks greased by'는 '~에 의해 순조롭게 진행되도록 만들어진 길'이라는 비유적 표현으로, 부정한 영향력이나 특혜를 통해 권력을 잡았다는 비판적 시각을 담고 있습니다. 'daddy's oil buddies'는 '아버지의 석유 재벌 친구들'이라는 비격식적이고 경멸적인 표현입니다.
> - **🔊 발음 & 연음 팁 (Pronunciation Tip)**: 'slithered into'는 '슬리더드-인투'처럼 연음되고, 'greased by'는 '그리스트 바이'로 발음합니다.
> - **📝 실전 예문 (Real Examples)**:
> - 🇺🇸 `He slithered into power through backroom deals.`
> - 🇰🇷 그는 뒷거래를 통해 권력을 잡았다.
> - 🇺🇸 `The politician slithered into office on tracks greased by corporate donations.`
> - 🇰🇷 그 정치인은 기업 기부금으로 깔아준 길을 타고 당선되었다.
>
> ### 🔹 12. `blistering follow-up to his smash #1 hit` : 그의 대히트작에 대한 맹렬한 후속작
> - **💡 뉘앙스 & 상황 맥락**: 'blistering'은 '맹렬한', '신랄한', '매서운'이라는 의미로, 비판의 강도나 작품의 열기를 강조합니다. 'smash #1 hit'는 '대히트작'을 뜻하며, 이전 작품의 성공을 언급하며 후속작에 대한 기대를 높입니다.
> - **🔊 발음 & 연음 팁 (Pronunciation Tip)**: 'blistering'은 '블리스터링'으로 발음하며, 'smash'와 'hit'에 강세를 줍니다.
> - **📝 실전 예문 (Real Examples)**:
> - 🇺🇸 `The band released a blistering follow-up to their debut album.`
> - 🇰🇷 그 밴드는 데뷔 앨범에 대한 맹렬한 후속작을 발표했다.
> - 🇺🇸 `Her new article is a blistering follow-up to her previous expose.`
> - 🇰🇷 그녀의 새 기사는 이전 폭로 기사에 대한 맹렬한 후속작이다.
>
> ### 🔹 13. `corporate barons who have bilked millions out of their employees' lifetime savings` : 직원들의 평생 저축액에서 수백만 달러를 사취한 기업 거물들
> - **💡 뉘앙스 & 상황 맥락**: 'corporate barons'는 '기업 거물들'을 의미하며, 종종 부정적인 뉘앙스로 사용됩니다. 'bilked millions out of'는 '수백만 달러를 사취하다', '속여 빼앗다'라는 뜻으로, 기업의 탐욕과 부도덕한 행위를 강하게 비판합니다.
> - **🔊 발음 & 연음 팁 (Pronunciation Tip)**: 'corporate barons'는 '코퍼릿 배런스'로 발음하고, 'bilked millions out of'는 '빌크트 밀리언스 아웃 오브'처럼 연음됩니다.
> - **📝 실전 예문 (Real Examples)**:
> - 🇺🇸 `The scam artists bilked millions out of unsuspecting investors.`
> - 🇰🇷 사기꾼들은 의심하지 않는 투자자들로부터 수백만 달러를 사취했다.
> - 🇺🇸 `He was accused of bilking his partners out of their share of the profits.`
> - 🇰🇷 그는 파트너들의 이익 지분을 사취한 혐의로 기소되었다.
>
> ### 🔹 14. `stripped away our civil liberties in the name of "homeland security"` : "국토 안보"라는 이름으로 우리의 시민 자유를 박탈했다
> - **💡 뉘앙스 & 상황 맥락**: 'strip away'는 '벗겨내다', '박탈하다'라는 의미로, 권리나 자유를 강제로 빼앗는 행위를 나타냅니다. 'civil liberties'는 '시민 자유'를, 'in the name of'는 '~의 이름으로'라는 뜻으로, 특정 명분을 내세워 부당한 행위를 정당화하는 상황을 비판할 때 사용됩니다. 'homeland security'는 9/11 이후 강조된 개념입니다.
> - **🔊 발음 & 연음 팁 (Pronunciation Tip)**: 'stripped away'는 '스트립트 어웨이'처럼 연음되고, 'in the name of'는 '인 더 네임 오브'로 자연스럽게 이어집니다.
> - **📝 실전 예문 (Real Examples)**:
> - 🇺🇸 `The new law stripped away some of the citizens' privacy rights.`
> - 🇰🇷 그 새로운 법은 시민들의 사생활 권리 일부를 박탈했다.
> - 🇺🇸 `Governments often strip away liberties in the name of national security.`

### 페이지 2

## Dude, Where's My Country?
**$24.95 FPT**
**($36.95 IN CANADA)**
When the powers-that-be succeeded in ignoring—and then silencing—the nation's widespread dissent over war, one man stood on an Oscar stage and, in front of a billion people, outed the commander in chief for his fictitious presidency and his fictitious war. Now, just a few months later, those words have remarkably become the accepted truth of the land.
Yes, **MICHAEL MOORE** is the scourge of Stupid White Men everywhere. He's taken on fat cats, gun nuts, lying politicians. The Guardian describes him as "a wake-up call, a kick in the mental backside." And now he's back—daring to ask the most urgent question of these perilous times:
**Dude, Where's My Country?**
**MICHAEL MOORE** is on a mission in his new book: Regime Change. The man who slithered into the White House on tracks greased by his daddy's oil buddies is one of many targets in Mike's blistering follow-up to his smash #1 hit *Stupid White Men*, the biggest-selling nonfiction book of the year. Now no one is safe: corporate barons who have bilked millions out of their employees' lifetime savings, legislators who have stripped away our civil liberties in the name of "homeland security," and even that right-wing brother-in-law of yours (yes, we all have one) who manages, year after year, through his babbling idiocy, to ruin Thanksgiving dinner.

**테러리즘을 멈추는 방법:**
"테러리스트가 되는 것을 멈춰라!"
**2004년에 부시를 추방하는 방법:**
"오프라를 출마시켜라!"
**마이크가 부시가 준 새로운 세금 감면액을 어떻게 써야 하는가:**
"이 책의 독자들이 내가 추가 수익으로 무엇을 해야 할지 결정하게 할 것이다!"
(www.SpendMikesTaxCut.com)
**9/11과 이라크 전쟁에 대한 모든 거짓말을 걸러내는 방법:**
"부시 씨, 당신이 한 번도 질문받지 못한 것 같은 몇 가지 질문이 있습니다..."
**보수 우파가 무엇인지 인식하는 방법:**
"죽어가는 종, 마지막 날에 몸부림치고 발버둥 치는."

두려움 없고, 재미있고, 격렬한 마이클 무어의 새 책은 우리가 모두 기다려온 무기입니다. 유머와 통찰력으로 시민들을 결집시키고 나라의 흐름을 바꾸는 그런 종류의 책입니다.
마이클 무어는 #1 뉴욕 타임즈 베스트셀러 『멍청한 백인 남자들(Stupid White Men)』과 『이것을 축소하라!(Downsize This!)』의 저자입니다. 그의 영화 『볼링 포 콜럼바인(Bowling for Columbine)』은 최우수 다큐멘터리 부문 아카데미 상을 수상했으며, 역대 가장 성공적인 다큐멘터리 영화 중 하나로 흥행 기록을 보유하고 있습니다. 그는 또한 고전 다큐멘터리 영화 『로저와 나(Roger & Me)』와 『더 빅 원(The Big One)』, 그리고 에미상 수상작인 TV 시리즈 『끔찍한 진실(The Awful Truth)』의 제작자이기도 합니다. 마이클 무어는 아내와 딸과 함께 미시간과 뉴욕 시에 살고 있습니다.

### 페이지 13

Introduction
I love listening to people’s stories about where they were and what
they were doing on the morning of 9/11, especially the stories from
the ones who, through luck or fate, were allowed to live.
For instance, there’s this guy who had just returned the day be-
fore from his honeymoon. That night, on September 10, his new
bride thought she’d make him her special homemade burrito. The
burrito was horrible, like eating tar stripped off the center line of
the Major Deegan expressway. But love ignores all of that and what
counts is the gesture, not the digestion. He told her how grateful he
was and how much he loved her. And he asked for another.
The next morning, September 11, 2001, he’s on the subway from
Brooklyn to his job on one of the top ﬂoors of the World Trade
Center. The subway might have been heading to Manhattan, but
the burrito was heading south, and I don’t mean the Jersey shore.
He starts to get sick, real sick, and decides to get off just one stop
before the World Trade Center. He runs up the subway stairs in a
desperate search for facilities. But this is New York and that was
not to be. And thus, on the corner of Park Row and Broadway, he
became a poster boy for Depends.
Embarrassed and humiliated—but feeling much better!—he
flagged down a gypsy cab and offered him a hundred dollars to
take him home ($9 for the ride, and $91 toward the price of a
new car).
When the man got home, he ran inside to take a shower and to

### 페이지 14

x
Introduction
put on a new set of clothes so he could get back to Manhattan.
Coming out of the shower he ﬂipped on the TV and, as he stood
there, he watched the plane slam right into the ﬂoor where he
worked, where he would have been right now had his loving wife
not made him that wonderful—that absolutely perfectly incredible
amazing . . . He broke down and began to cry.
My own 9/11 story wasn’t so close a call. I was asleep in Santa
Monica. The phone rang around 6:30 a.m. and it was my mother-
in-law. “New York is under attack!” is what I heard her say
through my half-awake ear. I wanted to say, “Yeah so what’s new—
and it’s 6:30 in the morning!”
“New York is at war,” she continued. This made no sense other
than, again, it always feels like war in New York. “Turn on the TV,”
she said. And so I did. I woke up my wife and as the television faded
on there were the towers, on ﬁre. We tried to call our daughter back
home in New York, no luck, then tried to call our friend Joanne
(who works near the World Trade Center), no luck, and then we just
sat there stunned. We didn’t leave the bed or the TV until ﬁve that
afternoon when we ﬁnally found out that our daughter and Joanne
were okay.
But a line producer we had just worked with, Bill Weems, was
not okay. As the networks started to run a scroll along the bottom
of the TV with the names of those who were on the planes, along
came Bill’s name on that screen. My last memory of him was the
two of us horsing around at a funeral home where we were shoot-
ing a piece about the tobacco industry. Put two guys with a dark
sense of humor around a bunch of undertakers and you’ve got
what we would call nirvana. Three months later he was dead
and—how do they say it?—“life as we knew it changed forever.”
Really? Did it? How has it changed? Is there enough distance from
that tragic day to ask that question and ﬁnd an intelligent answer?
Things certainly changed for Bill’s wife and his seven-year-old daugh-
ter. There’s the crime, right there, to have her daddy taken from her
at such a young age. And life changed for the loved ones of the other

### 페이지 15

xi
Introduction
3,000 who were murdered. They will never lose the sorrow they feel.
They are told that they “must move on.” Move on to where? Those
of us who have lost someone (and I guess that’s eventually everyone)
know that while life does “move on,” the sock in the gut, the sorrow
in the heart, will never leave, so ways must be found to embrace it
and make it work for you and the living.
Somehow we all work our way through our own personal losses
and we get up the next morning and the morning after that and ﬁx
the kids’ breakfast and do another load of laundry and pay the bills
and . . .
Meanwhile, in faraway Washington, D.C., life is changing, too.
Taking advantage of our grief, and our fear that “it” may happen
again, an appointed president uses the dead of 9/11 as a convenient
cover, a justiﬁcation, for permanently altering our American way of
life. Is that why they died, so that George W. Bush can turn the
country into Texas? We’ve already conducted two wars since 9/11,
and an upcoming third or a fourth is not all that unlikely. If this is
allowed to continue, then all we will have accomplished is to dis-
honor those 3,000-plus dead. I know Bill Weems didn’t die so he
could be used as an excuse to bomb innocents overseas. If his
death, his life, is to have a greater meaning from this moment for-
ward, it is to make sure that no one else like him will have to lose
his or her life in this insane, violent world, a world we now seem
hell-bent on running any way we damn well please.
I’m lucky, I guess, that I even get to write these words you are read-
ing. Not just because I get to live in the most wonderfulest country
in the whole wide world!, but because after 9/11, my former pub-
lisher, Regan Books (a division of HarperCollins which is a divi-
sion of the News Corp which owns Fox News and it’s all owned
by Rupert Murdoch), was trying its hardest to make sure my career
as an author would come to an early end.
The ﬁrst 50,000 copies of Stupid White Men came off the print-

### 페이지 16

xii
Introduction
ing press the day before 9/11, but when the tragedy struck the next
morning, the trucks that would carry them to the nation’s book-
stores never left the loading dock. The publisher then held the
books hostage for ﬁve long months—not simply out of good taste
and respect (which I might have been able to understand), but out
of a desire to censor me and the things I wanted to say. They in-
sisted I rewrite up to 50 percent of the book and that I remove sec-
tions that they found offensive to our leader, Mr. Bush.
I refused to change a word. A standoff ensued until a librarian in
New Jersey heard me talking about the phone call I had just re-
ceived from the Murdoch publisher telling me that it looked as if
they had no choice, thanks to my stubbornness, but to “pulp” and
recycle all 50,000 copies of my book that were gathering dust in a
warehouse in Scranton, Pennsylvania. I also was told by others not
to expect much in the way of a book career after this, as word
would spread that I was considered “trouble,” a royal pain in the
ass who wouldn’t play ball.
This librarian, Ann Sparanese, a woman I did not know, sent
out an e-mail to a list of librarians, telling them that my book was
being banned. Her letter shot around the Internet and, within
days, letters from angry librarians were ﬂooding Regan Books. I
got a call from the Murdoch police.
“What did you tell the librarians?”
“Huh? I don’t know any librarians.”
“Yes you do! You told them about what we are doing with your
book and now . . . we’re getting hate mail from librarians!”
“Hmm,” I replied, “I guess that’s one terrorist group you don’t
want to mess with.”
Fearing there would soon be a crazed mob of wild librarians
storming down Fifth Avenue and surrounding the HarperCollins
building, refusing to leave until either my book was liberated from
the Scranton warehouse or Murdoch himself was drawn and quar-
tered (though I would have settled for making Bill O’Reilly wear

### 페이지 17

xiii
Introduction
his underwear on his head for a week), the News Corp surren-
dered. They dumped my book in some bookstores with no adver-
tising, no reviews, and the offer of a three-city tour: Arlington!
Denver! Somewhere in New Jersey! In other words, the book was
sent to the gallows for a quick and painless death. It’s too bad you
wouldn’t listen to us, one Murdoch operative told me, we were
only trying to help you. The country is behind George W. Bush and
it is intellectually dishonest of you not to rewrite your book and
admit that he has done a good job since 9/11. You are out of touch
with the American people, and your book will now suffer as a re-
sult of it.
I was so out of touch with my fellow Americans that, within
hours after the book’s release, it went to number one on Ama-
zon—and within ﬁve days it had gone to its ninth printing. It’s in
its ﬁfty-second printing as I write this.
The worst thing to tell a free people in a country that’s still
mostly free is that they are not allowed to read something. That I
was able to be heard—and that my book would go on to be the
number-one selling nonﬁction hardcover book of the year in the
United States—screams volumes about this great country. The
people will not be intimidated and they will not be bullied by those
in charge. The American people may look like they don’t know
what’s going on half the time, and they may spend too much time
picking out different-colored covers for their cell phones, but
when push comes to shove, they’ll rise to the occasion and be there
for what is right.
So here I am now with this new book at none other than
AOLTIMEWARNER and Warner Books. I know, I know, when
will I learn my lesson? But here’s the good news. During the entire
time I’ve been writing this book, AOL has been trying to get rid
of Warner Books. Why would a media company want to get rid
of its book division? What did Warner Books do to upset the gods
of AOL? I ﬁgure if AOL wants to dump these guys, they have to

### 페이지 18

be okay. Plus, the other Warner folks in this tangled web—Warner
Bros. Pictures—are the people who distributed my ﬁrst ﬁlm,
Roger & Me. They were good and decent and they never threat-
ened to “pulp it.”
Okay, I’m rationalizing. Six media companies own everything.
Break up these monopolies for the good of the country! The free
ﬂow of news and information in a democracy must not be in the
hands of just a few rich men.
Yet, I have to say, they seem to be behind me here 100 percent.
1000 percent!! Not once have they said I was “trouble.”
But then, it’s not me they really need to worry about.
It’s the librarians.
And you.
Michael Moore
Somewhere over Greenland
August 15, 2003

### 페이지 21

AT FIRST, it seemed like a small plane had accidentally ﬂown
into the north tower of the World Trade Center. It was 8:46 a.m.
on September 11, 2001, and as the news leaked out across Amer-
ica, no one stopped what they were doing. It was a freaky occur-
rence to be sure, but most of the country carried on with getting
itself to work or school or back to sleep.1
Seventeen minutes later, reports came in that, now, a second
plane had hit the World Trade Center. Suddenly the collective
thinking of a nation shifted, in one fell swoop, to one single
thought: “This is no accident!”

### 페이지 22

TV sets everywhere were ﬂipped on. It was unlike anything you
had ever seen. Your brain, now confronted with an event for which
it had no prior reference, was scanning itself trying to ﬁgure out
what it all meant, and in particular, what it all meant for your own
personal survival, whether you were watching from your rooftop in
Tribeca or on CNN in Topeka.
You were in a daze, paralyzed in front of screen or a radio, then
you called everyone you knew, 290 million Americans all asking
each other the same question: What the hell is going on????
This was the first of many questions that arose about the
tragedy of September 11. Now, I’m not into conspiracy theories,
except the ones that are true or involve dentists. I believe that all
dentists must have gotten together at some point and decided
that the real money was in root canals and full sets of X-rays
every time you go in. No other mammal in the animal kingdom
has to go through this.
The questions I have about September 11 are not about how the
terrorists got past our defense system, or how they were able to
live in this country and never be detected, or how all Bulgarians
who worked at the WTC got a secret communiqué to not show up
to work that day, or how the towers came down so easily when
they were supposedly built to withstand earthquakes, tsunamis,
and truck bombs in their parking garage.
These were all questions that a special commission investigating
September 11 was supposed to answer. But the very formation of
that commission was opposed by the Bush administration and the
Republicans in Congress.2 Reluctantly, they ﬁnally agreed—but
then they sought to block the investigative body from doing its job
by stonewalling them on the evidence that they sought.3

The media watchdog group FAIR studied the evening newscasts
of six U.S. television networks and news channels for three weeks,
starting on March 20, 2003—the day after the U.S. bombing of
Iraq began. The study examined the afﬁliations and views of more
than 1,600 sources who appeared on-camera in stories about Iraq.
The results were hardly a surprise:
• Viewers were 25 times more likely to see a pro-war U.S.
source than someone with an anti-war point of view.

11. Bush Removal and Other Spring Cleaning Chores
In 2000, Gore received 48.38 percent of the vote and Ralph Nader got
2.74 percent, according to the ofﬁcial results from the Federal Election
Commission (www.fec.gov). Combined, this is 51.12 percent.
As The New York Times reported (David Rosenbaum, “Defying La-
bels Left or Right, Dean’s ’04 Run Is Making Gains,” July 30, 2003),
Dean “remains a ﬁscal conservative, he believes gun control should be
left to the states and he favors the death penalty for some crimes.”
According to the United States Census Bureau’s February 2002 report
“Voting and Registration in the Election of November 2000,” 42,359,000
white non-Hispanic men voted in the 2000 election. A total of 110,826,000
votes were cast, making white men 38.22 percent. Women of all races ac-
counted for 59,284,000 votes, or 53.49 percent. Black men cast 5,327,000
votes in 2000, comprising 4.81 percent, while Hispanic men cast 2,671,000
votes, equal to 2.41 percent—combined this is 7.22 percent. Thus, black
men, Hispanic men, and women totaled 60.71 percent of all voters.

Acknowledgments
I would like to thank my wife, Kathleen, who, when I didn’t feel like
writing, would turn up the radio and start dancing. Suddenly I
would ﬁnd the inspiration to keep writing and keep living.
I’d also like to thank my daughter, Natalie. She graduated from
college this year, something neither my wife nor I had done. We are
so proud of her.
I’d like to thank my sister Anne. She has contributed so much to
my work in the last two years, she has forgotten that she is still a
lawyer. When the last publisher wouldn’t organize a book tour, she
did—and drove us in the minivan from city to city. When my ﬁlm
debuted in Cannes, she became my de facto manager. When I needed
to nail certain parts of this book, she and her wonderful husband,
John Hardesty, were there till the bitter end. All this, and she’s the
middle child!
I’d like to thank the guy who invented the dill pickle. It is my
last remaining vice.
I’d like to thank my long-time buddy, Jeff Gibbs, who has bailed
me out on more than one occasion in recent years. He sat with me
looking up fact after fact while I wrote. He pushed me to keep the
book a-blazing when, at times, I just wanted to write haikus. This
guy is a genius. Though he had never worked on a movie before,
he produced some of the most memorable scenes in Bowling for
Columbine. Though he had never written a movie score, he scored
my ﬁlm in ﬁve days after the original music fell through. What’s
next? He’s scaring me.

### 페이지 271

About the Author
Michael Moore, who promised that, “barring success,” his last
book would deﬁnitely be his last, unfortunately had the success he
was avoiding and was forced to write this book. He has written the
bestsellers Downsize This: Random Threats from an Unarmed
American and, with Kathleen Glynn, Adventures in a TV Nation.
Spending ﬁfty-eight weeks on The New York Times bestseller lists,
his Stupid White Men became the largest-selling nonﬁction book
of 2002–03 in the U.S., with more than 4 million copies in print
worldwide. It won the “Book of the Year” award in Great Britain
and absolutely nothing in the United States, except there is a crazy
online petition for him to run for president. Michael Moore is the
Oscar-winning ﬁlmmaker of Bowling for Columbine and the
groundbreaking classic Roger & Me. He also is the Emmy-winning
writer/director/host of TV Nation and The Awful Truth. He has
failed to win a Grammy or a Tony but hopes to resolve this as soon
as he learns to sing and act. He recently lost ﬁfty-two pounds by re-
fusing to eat anything that says “nonfat” on it, and is working on
his next ﬁlm, titled Fahrenheit 9/11. He lives with his wife and
daughter in Michigan and New York.
